The chain rule, together with the negligible initial and final pieces, now gives <a id="eq:name-entropy-upper"></a>$$\label{eq:name-entropy-upper}
 \limsup_{i\to\infty}\frac1{N_i}
 H_{\xi_i}(\mathcal P_{[0,N_i]})
 \leq C_6\delta+\varepsilon+
 \frac{\log B_0+\log(C_0+1)}{M}.$$ All constants in the last numerator were fixed before $M$ was chosen. Choose $\delta$ first so that $C_6\delta<\eta/12$, then choose the truncation, charts and distance accuracy to make $\varepsilon<\eta/12$, and finally choose $M$ so large that the last term is less than $\eta/12$. Letting $i\to\infty$ contradicts <a href="#eq:name-entropy-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:name-entropy-lower">[eq:name-entropy-lower]</a>. This proves the proposition, including its subsequence formulation. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:combination"></a>
### Combining the root orientations

Proposition <a href="#prop:segment-activity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:segment-activity">25</a> guarantees activity somewhere on every long segment across each oriented cut. It does not yet assert that the roots are active at the same point. The wall invariance of the root indicators supplies the missing relation: on the scale $R_i$, an indicator can be read as a function of the value of its root alone. Transitivity then becomes an approximate addition rule for functions on the real line. We first obtain activity in every real interval and then use two-edge cycles to show that the inactive sets have measure zero.

<a id="stmt-thm-full-activity"></a>
<div id="thm:full-activity" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:full-activity"></a>
**Theorem 28** (Full activity). *For every ordered pair $j\ne k$ in $\{1,\ldots,6\}$, $$\label{eq:full-activity}
 \int_X g_{jk,i}\,\mathop{}\!d\mu_i\longrightarrow1.$$ Consequently every root is active almost surely in the limiting state law.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Functions of a root coordinate

<a id="eq:root-line-functions"></a>
For each root $\beta$, fix $b_\beta\in L$ such that $\beta(b_\beta)=1$. If $x$ is sampled with law $\mu_i$, define the random measurable functions $$\label{eq:root-line-functions}
 G_{\beta,i}^{x}(s)=g_{\beta,i}\bigl(xa(R_i s b_\beta)\bigr),
 \qquad s\in\mathbb R.$$ They take values in $\{0,1\}$. Regard them as elements of the compact metrizable space $$\mathcal B=\{f\in L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R):0\leq f\leq1\},$$ with weak-\* convergence on every bounded interval. More explicitly, one may use a countable dense set of $L^1$ test functions on each $[-q,q]$, $q\in\mathbb N$, to metrize this topology. The finite product $\mathcal B^{30}$ is also compact and metrizable. After passing to a subsequence, the laws of the arrays in <a href="#eq:root-line-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-line-functions">[eq:root-line-functions]</a> converge to a law of functions $(F_\beta)_\beta$ in this space. We also write $G_{jk,i}^{x}=G_{\beta_{jk},i}^{x}$ and $F_{jk}=F_{\beta_{jk}}$.

<a id="stmt-lem-root-line-laws"></a>
<div id="lem:root-line-laws" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29**. *For any such convergent subsequence, the limiting law and its prelimit arrays have the following properties.*

<a id="eq:positive-cut-integral"></a>
1.  *Almost surely, for every $v_0\in L$ with rational coordinates, every oriented cut $J$, and every positive rational $\tau$, $$\label{eq:positive-cut-integral}
      \sum_{\beta\in\mathcal E_J}\int_0^\tau
      F_\beta\bigl(\beta(v_0+t b_J)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!dt>0.$$*

<a id="eq:line-transitivity-defect"></a>
2.  *For distinct $j,k,\ell$, and every bounded rectangle $B\subset\mathbb R^2$, the nonnegative defects satisfy $$\label{eq:line-transitivity-defect}
      \mathbb E_{x\sim\mu_i}\int_B
      G_{jk,i}^{x}(s)G_{k\ell,i}^{x}(t)
      \bigl(1-G_{j\ell,i}^{x}(s+t)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!ds\,\mathop{}\!dt
      \longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-root-line-laws"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-root-line-laws</summary>

<a id="eq:common-translate-comparison"></a>
*Proof.* For $v$ in a fixed bounded subset of $L$, the vector $v-\beta(v)b_\beta$ belongs to $\ker\beta$ and remains bounded. Wall invariance <a href="#eq:wall-invariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wall-invariance">[eq:wall-invariance]</a>, followed by invariance of $\mu_i$ under the common translation, gives $$\label{eq:common-translate-comparison}
 \sup_{v\text{ in a fixed bounded set}}
 \int_X\left|
 G_{\beta,i}^{x}\bigl(\beta(v)\bigr)
 -g_{\beta,i}\bigl(xa(R_i v)\bigr)
 \right|\,\mathop{}\!d\mu_i(x)\longrightarrow0.$$

Fix the parameters in assertion 1. The left side of <a href="#eq:positive-cut-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-cut-integral">[eq:positive-cut-integral]</a> is a continuous function of the weak-\* array, because $\beta(b_J)\ne0$ for every crossing root. By <a href="#eq:common-translate-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-translate-comparison">[eq:common-translate-comparison]</a>, its prelimit differs in $L^1(\mu_i)$ by $o(1)$ from $$Y_i(x)=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal E_J}\int_0^\tau
 g_{\beta,i}\bigl(xa(R_i(v_0+t b_J))\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!dt.$$ Suppose that the limiting observable were zero on a set of probability $p>0$. Convergence in law and nonnegativity allow us to choose thresholds $\varepsilon_i\downarrow0$ and events $E_i$ with $\mu_i(E_i)\geq p/2$ such that the original prelimit observable is at most $\varepsilon_i$ on $E_i$. To see this directly, first choose a fixed sufficiently small continuity threshold, use convergence in law, and then decrease the threshold along a diagonal subsequence. The normalized restrictions $\mu_i|_{E_i}/\mu_i(E_i)$ consequently have $Y_i$ expectation tending to zero. Translate these probabilities by $a(R_i v_0)$ and call the results $\xi_i$. They satisfy $\xi_i\leq(2/p)\mu_i$, whereas the change of variable $u=R_i t$ gives $$\frac1{R_i}\int_0^{\tau R_i}\int_X
 A_{J,i}\bigl(xa(u b_J)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!d\xi_i(x)\,\mathop{}\!du
 \longrightarrow0.$$ This contradicts Proposition <a href="#prop:segment-activity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:segment-activity">25</a>. The parameters in assertion 1 range over a countable set, so the assertion holds simultaneously almost surely.

For assertion 2, the roots $\beta_{jk}$ and $\beta_{k\ell}$ are linearly independent. Choose $v(s,t)$ linearly in $(s,t)$ with $$\beta_{jk}(v(s,t))=s,\qquad
 \beta_{k\ell}(v(s,t))=t.$$ It follows that $\beta_{j\ell}(v(s,t))=s+t$. On a bounded rectangle, <a href="#eq:common-translate-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-translate-comparison">[eq:common-translate-comparison]</a> permits replacing all three functions in the defect by their indicators at the common point $xa(R_i v(s,t))$, with total expected error $o(1)$. The expected defect there is $o(1)$ by the approximate transitivity in Lemma <a href="#lem:activity-indicators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:activity-indicators">23</a>; its distribution is unchanged by the translation. Integrating over the rectangle proves the claim. ◻

</details>

The transitivity defect is nonlinear in the functions, so we do not pass it directly through weak-\* convergence. Instead we select deterministic arrays for which the defects themselves tend to zero.

<a id="stmt-lem-deterministic-root-arrays"></a>
<div id="lem:deterministic-root-arrays" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30**. *If <a href="#eq:full-activity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-activity">[eq:full-activity]</a> fails for some root, there is a subsequence and a deterministic choice of arrays $(G_{\beta,i})_\beta$, each taken from <a href="#eq:root-line-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-line-functions">[eq:root-line-functions]</a>, with the following properties:*

1.  *$G_{\beta,i}\to F_\beta$ weak-\* on bounded intervals, and the deterministic limiting functions satisfy <a href="#eq:positive-cut-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-cut-integral">[eq:positive-cut-integral]</a> for all its stated parameters;*

2.  *every defect in <a href="#eq:line-transitivity-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:line-transitivity-defect">[eq:line-transitivity-defect]</a>, without its expectation, tends to zero on every bounded rectangle;*

<a id="eq:limiting-deficiency"></a>
3.  *for some root $\beta_0$ and some bounded interval $I$ of positive length, $$\label{eq:limiting-deficiency}
      \int_I(1-F_{\beta_0}(s))\,\mathop{}\!ds>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-deterministic-root-arrays"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-deterministic-root-arrays</summary>

*Proof.* Pass to a subsequence on which $\int_X(1-g_{\beta_0,i})\,\mathop{}\!d\mu_i\geq\varepsilon$ for a fixed $\varepsilon>0$. Invariance of $\mu_i$ and Fubini’s theorem imply, for any fixed interval $I$, $$\mathbb E\int_I(1-G_{\beta_0,i}^{x}(s))\,\mathop{}\!ds
 =|I|\int_X(1-g_{\beta_0,i})\,\mathop{}\!d\mu_i
 \geq\varepsilon|I|.$$ The integral is a bounded weak-\* continuous observable, so the same inequality holds for the limiting law. This law therefore gives positive probability to a positive deficiency. Choose a point $F$ in its support that has a positive deficiency and satisfies assertion 1 of Lemma <a href="#lem:root-line-laws" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:root-line-laws">29</a>.

For each integer $q$, choose a neighborhood $U_q$ of $F$ of diameter less than $1/q$. It has positive limiting probability, and thus the prelimit probabilities of $U_q$ are bounded below by a positive number for all sufficiently large indices. There are finitely many root compositions. By <a href="#eq:line-transitivity-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:line-transitivity-defect">[eq:line-transitivity-defect]</a>, the sum of their defects on $[-q,q]^2$ has expectation tending to zero. Choose an index so large that, by Markov’s inequality, the probability that this sum exceeds $1/q$ is less than half the probability of $U_q$. There is therefore a sampled array in $U_q$ with total defect at most $1/q$. Make these choices with increasing indices. They converge to $F$ and have vanishing defects on every bounded rectangle, by nonnegativity and containment in some $[-q,q]^2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### From cut activity to every interval

For the rest of the argument, fix deterministic arrays as in Lemma <a href="#lem:deterministic-root-arrays" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:deterministic-root-arrays">30</a>. The labels are still $\{0,1\}$-valued; their weak-\* limits may initially take any value in $[0,1]$.

<a id="stmt-lem-positive-root-intervals"></a>
<div id="lem:positive-root-intervals" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31**. *For every root $\beta$ and every nonempty open interval $I\subset\mathbb R$, $$\int_I F_\beta(s)\,\mathop{}\!ds>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-positive-root-intervals"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-positive-root-intervals</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a root $\beta_{jk}$ and a desired interval $I$; replacing it by a bounded nonempty open subinterval only strengthens what we must prove. Choose a rational $v_0\in L$ so that $\beta_{jk}(v_0)$ lies in $I$. Choose $r>0$ small enough that $$[\beta_{jk}(v_0)-6r,\ \beta_{jk}(v_0)+6r]\subset I.$$ For every root $\alpha$, put $I_\alpha=(\alpha(v_0)-r,\alpha(v_0)+r)$. Draw the directed edge $p\to q$ if $\int_{I_{\beta_{pq}}}F_{pq}>0$.

This directed graph is strongly connected. Indeed, for any nonempty proper subset $J$, choose a positive rational $\tau$ sufficiently small that $\alpha(v_0+t b_J)\in I_\alpha$ for every $\alpha\in\mathcal E_J$ and $0\leq t\leq\tau$. Equation <a href="#eq:positive-cut-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-cut-integral">[eq:positive-cut-integral]</a> then supplies an edge from $J$ to its complement. A finite directed graph with an outgoing edge across every nontrivial cut is strongly connected: otherwise the set of vertices reachable from a suitable vertex is a nonempty proper set with no outgoing edge.

Take a simple directed path $j=p_0,p_1,\ldots,p_m=k$, where $m\leq5$. Write $\alpha_h=\beta_{p_{h-1}p_h}$ and $I_h=I_{\alpha_h}$. Weak-\* convergence gives positive lower bounds, for sufficiently large $i$, on $$\int_{I_h}G_{\alpha_h,i}(s)\,\mathop{}\!ds,
 \qquad 1\leq h\leq m.$$ The product of these integrals is consequently bounded below by a positive constant. Interpret it as the measure of tuples $(s_1,\ldots,s_m)\in\prod_h I_h$ on which all the path labels equal one.

Except for a set of tuple measure tending to zero, successive composition of these labels gives $$G_{p_0p_h,i}(s_1+\cdots+s_h)=1,
 \qquad 1\leq h\leq m.$$ Here is the integration detail. At step $h$, the failure of this implication, if all earlier implications held, is bounded by the transitivity defect for $p_0,p_{h-1},p_h$. The path is simple, so these are distinct vertices. Integration in $s_1,\ldots,s_{h-1}$ pushes Lebesgue measure on bounded intervals to a measure of bounded density in their sum. Multiplying by previous indicator restrictions can only decrease this measure. The defect integral on a fixed bounded rectangle therefore bounds the failure measure by a fixed multiple of an $o(1)$ quantity. Summing over the finitely many steps proves the assertion. If $m=1$, no composition is necessary.

The final sum $s_1+\cdots+s_m$ lies within $mr$ of $\alpha_1(v_0)+\cdots+\alpha_m(v_0)=\beta_{jk}(v_0)$, hence in $I$. Its pushforward density is bounded above by a fixed constant. Since a positive measure of tuples has final label one, we obtain $\liminf_i\int_I G_{jk,i}>0$. Weak-\* convergence proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

We have now located positive activity in every interval for every root. To eliminate a positive inactive set, we will translate an active set by sums of parameters from two opposite auxiliary roots. Composing successively with those two roots returns to the original root while adding their two parameters. Interval positivity supplies parameter sets of positive measure on both sides of zero. By mixing normalized Lebesgue measure on these sets, we will obtain bounded densities of mean zero, bounded support, and variance bounded away from zero. The sum of two independent parameters with these densities describes one cycle increment. For a fixed bounded interval $I$, the argument needs a fixed number of independent cycle increments to have density bounded below on $I-I$: this makes every part of the active set interact with every part of the inactive set inside $I$. The next lemma gives that lower bound uniformly over the densities that arise.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### A uniform convolution estimate

<a id="stmt-lem-convolution"></a>
<div id="lem:convolution" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32**. *Fix $B,M,v,L>0$. Consider the probability densities $p$ on $\mathbb R$ such that $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}p\subset[-B,B],\qquad 0\leq p\leq M,
 \qquad \int x p(x)\,\mathop{}\!dx=0,
 \qquad \int x^2p(x)\,\mathop{}\!dx\geq v.$$ There are an integer $m\geq2$ and $c>0$, depending only on $B,M,v,L$, such that $$p^{*m}(x)\geq c\qquad(|x|\leq L)$$ for every such density. The inequality uses the continuous representative of the convolution density.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-convolution"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-convolution</summary>

<a id="eq:uniform-characteristic-expansion"></a>
<a id="eq:small-frequency-domination"></a>
*Proof.* If the family is empty there is nothing to prove. Otherwise write $\sigma_p^2=\int x^2p(x)\,\mathop{}\!dx$, so $v\leq\sigma_p^2\leq B^2$, and let $$\phi_p(t)=\int e^{itx}p(x)\,\mathop{}\!dx.$$ Bounded support and the zero mean give the uniform expansion $$\label{eq:uniform-characteristic-expansion}
 \phi_p(t)=1-\frac12\sigma_p^2t^2+O(B^3|t|^3).$$ Applying the same expansion to $|\phi_p(t)|^2$, the characteristic function of the difference of two independent variables with density $p$, shows that for a sufficiently small $\delta>0$, depending only on $B,v$, $$\label{eq:small-frequency-domination}
 |\phi_p(t)|\leq e^{-vt^2/4}\qquad(|t|\leq\delta).$$

<a id="eq:large-frequency-gap"></a>
There is also a uniform gap below one outside this neighborhood. For $|t|\geq\delta$, an angle $\theta$, and $0<a<\pi$, the set $$\{x\in[-B,B]:\operatorname{dist}(tx-\theta,2\pi\mathbb Z)<a\}$$ has Lebesgue measure at most $a(2B/\pi+4/\delta)$. Indeed, each complete period contributes the fraction $a/\pi$ of its length, and at most two incomplete periods contribute at the ends. Choose $a$ so small that $M$ times this bound is at most $1/2$. With $\theta=\arg\phi_p(t)$, at least half of the probability lies outside this arc. Taking the real part after rotation by $e^{-i\theta}$ gives $$\label{eq:large-frequency-gap}
 \sup_p\sup_{|t|\geq\delta}|\phi_p(t)|
 \leq\frac{1+\cos a}{2}=:\rho<1.$$ If $\phi_p(t)=0$, the inequality is immediate and the angle is irrelevant. Notice that the estimate holds at arbitrarily large frequencies; no uniform version of the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma is needed.

<a id="eq:uniform-local-clt"></a>
Plancherel’s identity yields $$\int_\mathbb R|\phi_p(t)|^2\,\mathop{}\!dt
 =2\pi\int_\mathbb Rp(x)^2\,\mathop{}\!dx\leq2\pi M.$$ For $m\geq2$, $\phi_p^m$ is integrable. Fourier inversion therefore defines a continuous representative of $p^{*m}$. We claim the following uniform local central limit estimate: $$\label{eq:uniform-local-clt}
 \sup_p\sup_{x\in\mathbb R}
 \left|
 \sqrt m\,p^{*m}(x)
 -\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_p}
   \exp\left(-\frac{x^2}{2m\sigma_p^2}\right)
 \right|\longrightarrow0.$$ To verify it, change variables $u=t\sqrt m$ in the inversion formula. The difference in <a href="#eq:uniform-local-clt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-local-clt">[eq:uniform-local-clt]</a> is at most $1/(2\pi)$ times $$\int_\mathbb R\left|
 \phi_p(u/\sqrt m)^m-e^{-\sigma_p^2u^2/2}
 \right|\,\mathop{}\!du.$$ On each fixed bounded $u$ interval, the expansion <a href="#eq:uniform-characteristic-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-characteristic-expansion">[eq:uniform-characteristic-expansion]</a> proves uniform convergence of the integrand to zero; the logarithm may be taken near one, and its error is $O(B^3|u|^3/\sqrt m)$. On $|u|\leq\delta\sqrt m$, <a href="#eq:small-frequency-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-frequency-domination">[eq:small-frequency-domination]</a> bounds the first term by $e^{-vu^2/4}$, while the Gaussian term is bounded by $e^{-vu^2/2}$. Thus the contribution outside a large fixed bounded interval but inside this region is uniformly small. On $|u|>\delta\sqrt m$, <a href="#eq:large-frequency-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-frequency-gap">[eq:large-frequency-gap]</a> and Plancherel give $$\int_{|u|>\delta\sqrt m}|\phi_p(u/\sqrt m)|^m\,\mathop{}\!du
 \leq2\pi M\sqrt m\,\rho^{m-2}\longrightarrow0.$$ The Gaussian tail there also tends to zero uniformly. These three estimates prove <a href="#eq:uniform-local-clt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-local-clt">[eq:uniform-local-clt]</a>.

For $|x|\leq L$ and all sufficiently large $m$, the Gaussian term in <a href="#eq:uniform-local-clt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-local-clt">[eq:uniform-local-clt]</a> is at least $1/(2\sqrt{2\pi}B)$, uniformly in $p$. Increase $m$ so that the error is at most $1/(4\sqrt{2\pi}B)$. Then $$p^{*m}(x)\geq\frac1{4\sqrt{2\pi}B\sqrt m}
 \quad(|x|\leq L),$$ which is the required conclusion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Cycles remove the inactive sets

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-convolution-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-convolution</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:full-activity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-activity">28</a>.* Suppose the theorem fails, and take the deterministic arrays and limiting functions from Lemma <a href="#lem:deterministic-root-arrays" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:deterministic-root-arrays">30</a>. Fix an edge $j\to k$, and choose $\ell$ distinct from $j,k$. Lemma <a href="#lem:positive-root-intervals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:positive-root-intervals">31</a> supplies, for each of the roots $\ell j$ and $j\ell$, positive limiting integral on each of the intervals $(-2,-1)$ and $(1,2)$. Accordingly, for all sufficiently large $i$, each of the four sets where the relevant $G$ equals one has length bounded below by a positive constant.

For each auxiliary root, normalize Lebesgue measure on its two sets to probability densities $p_i^-$ and $p_i^+$. Their means belong to $[-2,-1]$ and $[1,2]$, respectively. There is a convex combination $$p_i=\lambda_i p_i^-+(1-\lambda_i)p_i^+$$ with mean zero. The coefficients can be chosen in $[1/3,2/3]$ by solving the mean-zero equation. Thus these densities have support in $[-2,2]$, a common finite upper bound, and variance at least one. They are supported entirely where the corresponding auxiliary indicator is one. Denote the density for $\ell j$ by $p_i$ and that for $j\ell$ by $q_i$.

<a id="eq:one-cycle-error"></a>
Starting from $G_{jk,i}(t)=1$, composition with a parameter $s$ in the support of $p_i$ gives the edge $\ell k$, and composition with a parameter $s'$ in the support of $q_i$ returns to $jk$: $$\ell j+jk=\ell k,\qquad j\ell+\ell k=jk.$$ Both are root compositions with three distinct vertices. Their defect estimates imply, for every fixed bounded interval $I$, $$\label{eq:one-cycle-error}
 \int_I\int_\mathbb R\int_\mathbb R
 G_{jk,i}(t)\bigl(1-G_{jk,i}(t+s+s')\bigr)
 p_i(s)q_i(s')\,\mathop{}\!ds\,\mathop{}\!ds'\,\mathop{}\!dt\longrightarrow0.$$ Indeed, except on null sets of parameters, both auxiliary labels are one. The indicator in <a href="#eq:one-cycle-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-cycle-error">[eq:one-cycle-error]</a> is then bounded by the sum of the two composition defects. For the first defect, integrate out $s'$ and use the uniform density bound on $p_i$. For the second, the variable $t+s$ has a bounded density on a fixed bounded interval when $t\in I$ is integrated with Lebesgue measure and $s$ with $p_i$. The bound on $q_i$ and the defect estimate for $(j\ell,\ell k)$ finish the verification.

<a id="eq:repeated-cycle-error"></a>
Put $r_i=p_i*q_i$. These cycle-increment densities have support in $[-4,4]$, a common density bound, mean zero, and variance at least two. We can iterate <a href="#eq:one-cycle-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-cycle-error">[eq:one-cycle-error]</a> any fixed number $m$ of times to get $$\label{eq:repeated-cycle-error}
 \int_I\int_\mathbb R
 G_{jk,i}(t)\bigl(1-G_{jk,i}(t+u)\bigr)
 r_i^{*m}(u)\,\mathop{}\!du\,\mathop{}\!dt\longrightarrow0.$$ For clarity, along a sequence of $m$ increments, a path starting with label one and ending with label zero has a first transition from one to zero. Bound its indicator by the sum of the indicators of these transitions. The intermediate positions lie in $I+[-4m,4m]$. After integrating all preceding increments, their distributions have densities bounded by one relative to Lebesgue measure on that larger interval, because the initial $t$ is integrated over $I$ and all increments have probability laws. Each term is therefore bounded by the one-cycle error on the larger interval. There are only $m$ terms, proving <a href="#eq:repeated-cycle-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-cycle-error">[eq:repeated-cycle-error]</a>.

Fix a bounded interval $I$ of positive length and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:convolution">32</a> to the $r_i$, with $L$ large enough that $I-I\subset[-L,L]$. Choose its fixed power $m$ and lower bound $c>0$. In <a href="#eq:repeated-cycle-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-cycle-error">[eq:repeated-cycle-error]</a>, retain only the pairs $t,t+u\in I$. We obtain $$c\left(\int_I G_{jk,i}(t)\,\mathop{}\!dt\right)
   \left(\int_I(1-G_{jk,i}(t))\,\mathop{}\!dt\right)
 \longrightarrow0.$$ The first factor has positive lower limit by Lemma <a href="#lem:positive-root-intervals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:positive-root-intervals">31</a>. Hence $\int_I(1-G_{jk,i})\to0$ for every such interval. Weak-\* convergence shows that $F_{jk}=1$ almost everywhere. The argument applies to every root, contradicting the deficiency <a href="#eq:limiting-deficiency" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-deficiency">[eq:limiting-deficiency]</a>. Thus <a href="#eq:full-activity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-activity">[eq:full-activity]</a> holds.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:activity-indicators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:activity-indicators">23</a>, the sequence indicators converge jointly in law to the activity indicators of the limiting state. Since each has expectation tending to one, every limiting root indicator equals one almost surely. There are only thirty roots, so this holds for all of them simultaneously. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Proof of the equidistribution theorem

We now return to the packets of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:cusp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cusp">4</a> and Mahler’s compactness criterion give tightness. Take an arbitrary subsequence with a weak probability limit $\mu$. It is $A$-invariant, and Proposition <a href="#prop:ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:ball">5</a> gives the ordinary-ball estimate required by Proposition <a href="#prop:positive-entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:positive-entropy">8</a>. Theorem <a href="#thm:ekl" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ekl">9</a> therefore makes almost every $A$-ergodic component of $\mu$ homogeneous.

Apply the intermediate-conditioning construction to this subsequence, passing further to a subsequence wherever necessary. The augmented limiting state still projects to $\mu$ on $X$. Proposition <a href="#prop:bowen" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bowen">7</a> supplies the tube estimate throughout the required time interval. Hence Theorem <a href="#thm:full-activity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-activity">28</a> makes every root active almost surely in this state.

If proper homogeneous components had positive total weight, Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-pieces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-pieces">11</a> would place positive $\mu$-mass on the countable union of their fixed-point pieces. At least one piece would have positive mass. Its fixing diagonal is nonidentity, so it has two unequal diagonal entries. The corresponding root plaques meet that piece in at most countably many points. By Lemma <a href="#lem:transverse-inactive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transverse-inactive">24</a>, that root is inactive almost surely on states projecting to the piece, a contradiction. Thus almost every component is the $G$-invariant probability, and $\mu=m_6$.

Every weakly convergent subsequence supplied by tightness has the same limit. It follows that the original sequence converges to $m_6$ on $C_c(X_6)$. Proposition <a href="#prop:cusp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cusp">4</a> already gives the compact sets required for the nonescape assertion. The estimates are uniform in the embedding ordering, so the conclusion holds for the arbitrary choices in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The two different comparisons in the product rule. Each input picture fixes a $q$-cell at depth $F$; the coordinate $q$ is suppressed. Dashed outlines denote coarse output transverse cells; thin solid lines denote fine transverse cells. Dotted lines denote fixed $U$ bins in (a) and stage-$(k+j)$ $W$ subtiles in (b). Shading marks a labelled subpatch, not a density. In (a), contraction permits one coarse transverse-cell comparison for the whole patch and its subpatches. In (b), expansion leaves the full $U$ interval broad. The full patch is compared with $V$ data and the thin $u$-subpatch with $W$ data; the information estimate compares their descendant-tile laws and hence their resolved $W$ tests. All widths and tile shapes are schematic: $W$ can have several dimensions and its tiles need not be rectangles. The resulting factor identifications are projective.",
      "figure_id": "fig:product-mechanism",
      "pdf_page": 32,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:product-mechanism",
          "exact_quote": "The two different comparisons in the product rule. Each input picture\nfixes a $q$-cell at depth $F$; the coordinate $q$ is suppressed. Dashed outlines\ndenote coarse output transverse cells; thin solid lines denote fine transverse\ncells. Dot",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1cm,y=1cm,\n  font=\\small,\n  >={Stealth[length=2mm]},\n  patch/.style={draw=black!75,line width=.55pt,fill=black!4},\n  selected/.style={draw=blue!65!black,line width=.55pt,fill=blue!16},\n  coarse/.style={draw=black!70,dashed,line width=.65pt},\n  fine/.style={draw=black!35,line width=.25pt},\n  bins/.style={draw=black!70,densely dotted,line width=.5pt},\n  note/.style={align=left,font=\\footnotesize},\n  leader/.style={draw=black!65,line width=.4pt,->}\n]\n\\path[use as bounding box] (0,-5.15) rectangle (14.2,4.35);\n\n\\node[anchor=west,font=\\small\\bfseries] at (0,4.1)\n  {(a) Contract $W$: identify the $U$ factor and prove independence};\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (2.35,3.67)\n  {one $q$-cell; full $w,u$ ranges};\n\\draw[patch] (.65,1.3) rectangle (4.05,3.35);\n\\draw[selected] (2.15,1.3) rectangle (2.75,3.35);\n\\node[font=\\footnotesize,anchor=north] at (2.45,1.19) {$w$-cell};\n\\node[anchor=east] at (.51,3.2) {$u$};\n\\node[anchor=north] at (3.9,1.19) {$w$};\n\n\\draw[->,line width=.8pt] (4.65,2.35) -- (7.1,2.35);\n\\node[above] at (5.87,2.45) {$a_0^{t}$, $t\\asymp T$};\n\\node[below,font=\\footnotesize,align=center] at (5.87,2.18)\n  {$W$ contracts; $U$ is fixed\\\\$T\\prec F$};\n\n\\draw[coarse] (7.95,1.15) rectangle (10.15,3.5);\n\\draw[patch] (8.55,1.3) rectangle (9.55,3.35);\n\\draw[selected] (8.991,1.3) rectangle (9.168,3.35);\n\\foreach \\x in {8.6,8.7,8.8,8.9,9.0,9.1,9.2,9.3,9.4,9.5}\n  \\draw[fine] (\\x,1.3) -- (\\x,3.35);\n\\foreach \\y in {1.78,2.31,2.84}\n  \\draw[bins] (7.95,\\y) -- (10.15,\\y);\n\\node[font=\\footnotesize] at (9.05,3.73) {output $U$ chart};\n\\node[note,anchor=west] at (10.58,3.18)\n  {One coarse transverse cell\\\\contains the whole patch.};\n\\draw[leader] (10.5,3.15) -- (10.17,3.15);\n\\node[note,anchor=west] at (10.58,2.26)\n  {Fine transverse cells\\\\resolve the $w$-label.};\n\\draw[leader] (10.5,2.24) -- (9.28,2.24);\n\\node[note,anchor=west] at (10.58,1.42) {Common $U$ bins};\n\\draw[leader] (10.5,1.48) -- (10.04,1.78);\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (7.1,.5)\n  {Mesh comparison: revealing the $w$-cell changes the $U$-bin law by $o(1)$ in sampling mean.};\n\n\\node[anchor=west,font=\\small\\bfseries] at (0,-.22)\n  {(b) Expand a $W$ tile: identify the remaining $W$ factor};\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (2.35,-.86)\n  {one stage-$k$ tile\\\\shape $\\Delta^k\\mathcal F$};\n\\draw[patch] (2.0,-3.7) rectangle (2.7,-1.55);\n\\draw[selected] (2.0,-2.68) rectangle (2.7,-2.43);\n\\foreach \\x in {2.175,2.35,2.525}\n  \\draw[bins] (\\x,-3.7) -- (\\x,-1.55);\n\\node[anchor=east] at (1.86,-1.72) {$u$};\n\\node[anchor=north] at (2.65,-3.81) {$w$};\n\\node[note,anchor=east] at (1.72,-2.57) {$u$-cell\\\\depth $S$};\n\\draw[leader] (1.8,-2.57) -- (1.98,-2.57);\n\n\\draw[->,line width=.8pt] (3.65,-2.57) -- (6.5,-2.57);\n\\node[above] at (5.08,-2.45) {$a_0^{-(k+m_0)}$};\n\\node[below,font=\\footnotesize,align=center] at (5.08,-2.73)\n  {$W$ expands; $U$ is fixed\\\\$S\\prec T\\prec F$, $k\\asymp T$};\n\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (8.63,-.88)\n  {fixed output shape\\\\$\\Delta^{-m_0}\\mathcal F$};\n\\draw[patch] (7.05,-3.7) rectangle (10.2,-1.55);\n\\draw[selected] (7.05,-2.68) rectangle (10.2,-2.43);\n\\foreach \\x in {7.8375,8.625,9.4125}\n  \\draw[bins] (\\x,-3.7) -- (\\x,-1.55);\n\\draw[coarse] (6.9,-2.92) rectangle (10.35,-2.2);\n\\foreach \\y in {-2.63,-2.58,-2.53,-2.48}\n  \\draw[fine] (7.05,\\y) -- (10.2,\\y);\n\\node[note,anchor=west] at (10.58,-1.94)\n  {Only the $u$-subpatch fits\\\\in one coarse transverse\\\\cell for the $W$ chart.};\n\\draw[leader] (10.5,-2.02) -- (10.35,-2.2);\n\\node[note,anchor=west] at (10.58,-3.18)\n  {Fine transverse cells\\\\resolve the $u$-label.};\n\\draw[leader] (10.5,-3.05) -- (9.72,-2.6);\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (8.63,-4.04)\n  {Stage-$(k+j)$ subtiles resolve $W$ tests.};\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize,text width=14cm] at (7.1,-4.78)\n  {Information comparison: an $O(S)$ budget over $T$ stages makes the $u$-label change\\\\\n   the descendant-tile law by $o(1)$ in sampling mean, at a suitable stage $k\\asymp T$.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Schematic diagram showing two comparison procedures, labeled (a) and (b):\n- (a) Contract W: On the left, a square/rectangular cell labeled \"one q-cell; full w, u ranges\" with vertical axis u and horizontal axis w, containing a shaded vertical strip labeled \"w-cell\". A horizontal arrow points to the right box, labeled with action \"$a_0^t, t \\asymp T$\" above and \"$W\\text{ contracts; } U\\text{ is fixed}$\", \"$T \\prec F$\" below. The right box is labeled \"output U chart\", enclosed in a dashed rectangular boundary annotated \"One coarse transverse cell contains the whole patch.\" It contains horizontal dotted lines annotated \"Common U bins\" and vertical thin solid lines annotated \"Fine transverse cells resolve the w-label.\" Text below reads: \"Mesh comparison: revealing the w-cell changes the U-bin law by o(1) in sampling mean.\"\n- (b) Expand a W tile: On the left, a box labeled \"one stage-k tile\" and \"shape $\\Delta^k F$\" with vertical axis u and horizontal axis w, containing a horizontal shaded strip labeled \"u-cell depth S\". A horizontal arrow points right, labeled with \"$a_0^{-(k+m_0)}$\" above and \"$W\\text{ expands; } U\\text{ is fixed}$\", \"$S \\prec T \\prec F, k \\asymp T$\" below. The right box is labeled \"fixed output shape $\\Delta^{-m_0} F$\", showing a thin horizontal dashed box containing the u-subpatch annotated \"Only the u-subpatch fits in one coarse transverse cell for the W chart.\", vertical thin lines annotated \"Fine transverse cells resolve the u-label.\", and horizontal dotted lines annotated below by \"Stage-(k + j) subtiles resolve W tests.\" Text below reads: \"Information comparison: an O(S) budget over T stages makes the u-label change the descendant-tile law by o(1) in sampling mean, at a suitable stage $k \\asymp T$.\""
    }
